# Backrooms Sitcom Audition Prompt Pack — FULLY MERGED, CAMERA-LOGIC CORRECTED — 2026-09-08

Self-contained replacement for the original pack and for Backrooms_Sitcom_Audition_Prompt_Pack_CORRECTED.md (that file's patches are now baked directly into every clip below; keep it only as a changelog).

## Camera modes used throughout

- **A — Tripod** (C001-C014): fixed camera, actor fully visible, ordinary third-person audition framing.

- **FALL** (C015): camera tumbles with the actor mid-fall.

- **B — Handheld POV** (C016-C062): camera in the actor's hand, forward-facing. The actor's face/body is never shown — only hand/forearm at frame edge, shadow, and environment.

- **C — Propped** (C063-C102): the camcorder has been set down, wedged, or propped by the actor; stable third-person coverage of both the actor and the entity, still 100% diegetic (never an outside camera).


Every clip below states its mode. Universal negative prompt and continuity anchors are shared across all clips and given once here to avoid repetition:


**Universal negative prompt (append to every still and video prompt):**

No subtitles, captions, logos, watermark, readable text, extra limbs, duplicate people, face changes, costume changes, melted hands, fused fingers, warped limbs, floating objects, disappearing objects, duplicate camera, duplicate entity, flickering geometry, texture swimming, crawling wallpaper patterns, unstable lighting, frame tearing, broken perspective, impossible shadows, random room changes, accidental jump cuts, cartoon style, glossy commercial lighting, clean digital cinema, smooth gimbal movement, fantasy architecture, excessive fog, gore, blood, exaggerated monster face.


**Universal continuity anchors (append to every still):**

Use the same actor, entity design if present, camcorder look, yellow Level 0 architecture, fluorescent lighting, damp carpet, and restrained horror-comedy tone established by the source pack.


**Universal overall_soundscape (every video prompt):**

Built-in camcorder microphone audio with steady tape hiss, room tone, fluorescent hum, footsteps, breath, fabric movement, carpet contact, camera handling noise, and the physical sounds implied by the visible action. Dialogue is synchronized to the visible speaker and remains short enough for the clip.

**non_diegetic_music:** N/A


---

## SECTION 1 — SDXL START/LAST FRAME IMAGE PROMPTS


### C001 — START FRAME IMAGE — 0:00-0:10 — Camera Mode: A

Filename: `clip_001_start.png`

Model target: SDXL still image

Positive prompt:

Authentic 1990s consumer camcorder found-footage horror-comedy, 4:3 image, sharp static reference frame (no motion blur, no autofocus hunting, no exposure pumping — those belong only in the video prompt), mild VHS softness, practical fluorescent or household lighting, realistic architecture, restrained color, believable physical weight, no polished cinematic camera moves, no modern digital sharpness. Preserve character identity, clothing, camera damage, lighting direction, room geometry, and screen direction between frames. The comedy stays understated and deadpan until the entity begins mirroring the actor. Static single-frame SDXL reference still, 4:3 found-footage composition, clean readable handoff frame for image-to-video.

Diegetic camcorder POV lock — Mode A (tripod): the image must look like it was captured only by the story's physical consumer camcorder, fixed on a tripod. The actor is fully, ordinarily visible in third-person audition framing. Do not use an outside film camera, drone, gimbal, impossible reverse angle, or omniscient camera.

Static wide view of a cheap apartment living room converted into a makeshift audition space, camcorder on tripod, actor standing on a taped mark, warm household lamp and flat ceiling light.

Preserve all character identity, clothing, camera defects, room geometry, lighting direction, screen direction, and Backrooms continuity exactly.

Negative prompt: see Universal negative prompt above.

Continuity anchors: see Universal continuity anchors above.

## APPENDIX — GLOBAL CONTINUITY AND ASSEMBLY NOTES

### Character Bible

**The actor.** Male sitcom actor, late 20s, average build, expressive but ordinary face, short messy dark-brown hair, faint five-o'clock shadow, olive-green overshirt over a faded gray T-shirt, dark straight-leg jeans, worn white sneakers, cheap digital watch on left wrist. He is confident and performative at first, then privately anxious, then increasingly curious and playful. His humor is accidental and understated.

**The entity.** Very tall, extremely thin humanoid silhouette, approximately 2.2 meters tall, matte charcoal-black surface with no visible clothing, long arms hanging too low, narrow shoulders, slightly oversized head, facial features difficult to resolve because the image never fully focuses on it. Its movement is quiet and almost human but delayed by a fraction of a second. It should feel strange before it feels threatening. Later it mirrors the actor exactly.

**The camera.** Small consumer camcorder, on a cheap tripod at the audition (Mode A), then handheld by the actor (Mode B), then set down/propped (Mode C). Wide-angle lens, slightly soft image, occasional tape warble, built-in microphone, weak automatic exposure, timestamp absent, no visible brand. When the camera falls (C015), the image briefly points at carpet and ceiling before recovering.

### Story Rules

- The first quarter (C001-C014) is played completely straight as found footage, Mode A.
- The transition happens at C015, not immediately.
- The entity is not clearly visible until after C047-048, and not fully confronted until C063.
- The chase (C051-C062) is frightening because the entity sounds almost human and says ordinary things.
- The apparent attack (C068-C069) ends in static, then the camera comes back as though the actor is opening his eyes (C072-073) — this is the pivot from Mode B to Mode C, since the camera is now on the ground/propped rather than in his hand.
- The comedy begins through awkward behavior (C076 onward), not a sudden change into cartoon lighting.
- The entity never becomes cute. Its mirroring is funny because the situation remains unsettling.

### Backrooms Lore Basis

The Backrooms has no single universal canon. These lines use concepts documented in the Wikidot community canon and present uncertain material as theories, not facts:
- The Backrooms are an alternate dimension outside baseline reality.
- Noclipping is passing through reality itself, often through accidental collisions or environmental thresholds.
- Levels are locations, not necessarily literal floors.
- Noclipping destinations are unpredictable, and the same kind of threshold can lead to different places.
- Level 0 has an Isolation Effect that can prevent people who entered together from finding one another.
- Entities are not uniformly hostile; the community FAQ explicitly allows entities that are dangerous, friendly, or unusually human.
- Some lore theories describe the Backrooms as a four-dimensional superposition or as a hypercomplex simulation/test environment.
- One documented theory suggests some entities may be organisms that entered long ago and evolved within the Backrooms.

Sources: Wikidot FAQ (backrooms-wiki.wikidot.com/faq), Noclipping and its theories (backrooms-wiki.wikidot.com/phenomene-1-fr), Level 0 (backrooms-wiki.wikidot.com/level-0).

### Dialogue Tone for the Friendly Entity

The friendly entity should sound sincerely helpful, slightly literal, and socially inexperienced. It is not a comedian and does not understand why its answers are funny. The actor supplies the comedy through normal human questions, awkward skepticism, and misplaced optimism. The phrase "Come back" and later "What's weird?" (C101) are the entity's ordinary, unremarkable questions that land as unsettling because of context, not delivery.

### Generation Notes for the Static Reset (C069)

Make the static cut visually abrupt but short. Do not regenerate the attack as a complete action scene. The audience should understand that something happened while still feeling that the recording lost the important part. The following shot (C070-072) should be calmer, brighter, and more conversational while preserving the same yellow Level 0 architecture and camcorder defects. Under the corrected camera-mode system, this is also the exact point where the story transitions from Mode B (handheld) to Mode C (propped) — the camera is knocked loose during the attack and never picked back up.

### Assembly Notes — Corrected

Use the same character reference, entity reference, camera reference, aspect ratio, seed, and visual-style block for every generation. Generate the stills first and approve each handoff before animating. Use the previous clip's approved last frame as the next clip's start frame. Keep each clip's final 1.5 seconds calm enough to function as a clean handoff. For dialogue clips, keep the spoken sentence short enough to fit naturally inside ten seconds and avoid rapid mouth movement. Use lower motion strength for walking, conversation, and exploration; reserve stronger motion for the fall, chase, entanglement, and final impact. If a clip contains artifacts, regenerate that clip alone rather than changing neighboring clips. Do not add a new visual style, lens, camera angle, character design, or room type unless the story explicitly calls for it.

**New:** Confirm each clip's Camera Mode (A / B / C / FALL, stated at the top of every prompt in this document) before rendering. Reject and regenerate any Mode B still that shows the actor's face or torso — that is the one hard rule this corrected pack exists to enforce. Mode C stills should look stable, not handheld-shaky, consistent with a propped camera. Mode A and the C015 fall need no change from how they were originally generated.
